The aspartate accepts a proton from the substrate&#x27;s hydroxyl group during the cleavage step, functioning as the proton acceptor. Glutamate and histidine are not the primary proton acceptors in this well-characterized catalytic mechanism. Since the question specifically asks for the proton **acceptor**, the answer is aspartate.<br><br>2. answer: D</td>

In some organisms, particularly bacteria, the enzymes involved in both fatty acid β-oxidation and ketone body metabolism/synthesis are well-connected. The 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase (HAD) catalyzes:
- In β-oxidation: 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA + NAD+ → 3-ketoacyl-CoA + NADH
- In ketone body metabolism: 3-hydroxybutyryl-CoA + NAD+ → acetoacetyl-CoA + NADH (reverse of thiolase + β-oxidation step)

Wait, actually in ketone body SYNTHESIS:
- Acetyl-CoA → Acetoacetyl-CoA (thiolase)
- Acetoacetyl-CoA → HMG-CoA (HMG-CoA synthase)
- HMG-CoA → Acetoacetate (HMG-CoA lyase)
- Acetoacetate → β-hydroxybutyrate (β-hydroxybutyrate dehydrogenase, uses NADH)

So the enzyme connecting both pathways would be:
- β-hydroxybutyrate dehydrogenase (ketone body synthesis) and 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase (β-oxidation) - these are different enzymes but closely related (same superfamily)

But if we&#x27;re talking about a SINGLE enzyme that participates in BOTH pathways, we need to find one enzyme that is genuinely part of both.

In ketone body UTILIZATION (not synthesis):
- Acetoacetate → Acetoacetyl-CoA (succinyl-CoA:acetoacetate CoA-transferase)
- Acetoacetyl-CoA → 2 Acetyl-CoA (thiolase)

In fatty acid β-oxidation (even-numbered fatty acids):
- Acyl-CoA → trans-Δ²-enoyl-CoA (acyl-CoA dehydrogenase, uses FAD)
- trans-Δ²-enoyl-CoA → 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA (enoyl-CoA hydratase)
- 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA → 3-ketoacyl-CoA (3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase, uses NAD+)
- 3-ketoacyl-CoA → Acyl-CoA (n-2) + Acetyl-CoA (thiolase)

So HAD is NOT directly in ketone body synthesis or utilization. But it IS related (same superfamily).

Actually, some HAD enzymes can act on 3-hydroxybutyryl-CoA, which IS an intermediate in odd-chain fatty acid oxidation or in the reverse of ketone body utilization. So there might be a connection.

The HMG-CoA dehydrogenase (in ketone body synthesis) and the 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase (in β-oxidation) are in the same superfamily but are different enzymes.
